<!-- README.md -->
# hi2: quantizer for the rdna_hint_flex weight format

`hi2` converts a local Hugging Face model into a **rdna_hint_flex** checkpoint.
Quantization stores weight rows as 2-bit or 4-bit integers (INT2 or INT4), with
8-bit integers (INT8) available on request. The format adds block parameters,
an optional smoothing vector, and an order-16 Hadamard rotation. It targets AMD
RDNA2 GPUs (gfx1030). The [published measurements](docs/results.md) apply to
specific models and runtime configurations, not every backend.

Choose an allocation-error budget or a target bit rate. The tool assigns fewer
bits to less sensitive rows. Then measure the checkpoint on independent text:
an allocation target is not a model-quality guarantee.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Local model + calibration text] --> B[1 Tabulate<br/>statistics, error tables]
    B --> C[2 Allocate<br/>INT2 / INT4 per row]
    C --> D[3 Encode<br/>error-aware rounding]
    D --> E[4 Distill<br/>layer-wise QAD + optional final-KL]
    E --> F[rdna_hint_flex checkpoint]
```

## Start here

1. **Inputs and quantization.** [Install](docs/installation.md), then
   [check model eligibility](docs/getting-started.md#supported-inputs-and-evidence).
   Use a local Hugging Face safetensors directory, not a remote model ID.
   [Prepare separate calibration, tuning and final-test data](docs/evaluation.md#prepare-independent-data).
2. **Validation scope.** [Check structure, size and dense numerical quality](docs/evaluation.md)
   separately. Structural validation does not establish model quality.
   Dense evaluation does not qualify a native low-bit runtime.
3. **Execution and recovery.** Start with the [no-download CPU tutorial](docs/getting-started.md#executed-no-download-cpu-smoke),
   then follow the [first-model walkthrough](docs/getting-started.md#first-model-walkthrough).
   Choose explicit devices and plan resources before a full run.
   Keep the source, data and work paths for [safe recovery](docs/operations.md#resume-and-invalidation).
   Choose [dense export or a separately qualified native runtime](docs/getting-started.md#export-and-native-serving)
   only after validation.

With calibration, the normal recipe starts with **post-training quantization
(PTQ)**. It then uses **quantization-aware distillation (QAD)** to train quantized
layers against the original model. The `default` preset also requests
**final-KL**, a phase that aims to reduce Kullback–Leibler divergence between model
output distributions. Neither allocation nor QAD guarantees final divergence (KLD).
`--no-qad` selects PTQ only. Final-KL can be disabled or skipped if the model does
not fit; check the phase messages.

[usage.md](docs/usage.md) is the canonical command-line interface (CLI) reference.
`python -m hi2 ...` and `hi2 ...` use the same entry point.

## Status

Dense PTQ/QAD and per-expert mixture-of-experts (MoE) PTQ paths are implemented.
Their measured coverage differs. Consult the
[support matrix](docs/getting-started.md#supported-inputs-and-evidence).
The OLMoE measurements do not establish support for MoE QAD, shared-expert
variants, or fused 3-D expert weights on disk.

Native HIP kernels and the HI2-enabled vLLM loader live outside this repository.
Published serving results are historical evidence; this checkout does not provide
a version-pinned, newly validated native installation recipe. Dense numerical
evaluation is not native-loader validation.

## Development tests

Follow the [installation guide](docs/installation.md#checkout-and-virtual-environment)
for the test and optional MCP extras. Run from the checkout with that interpreter:

```bash
python -m pytest -q tests
```

Most tests run on CPU; accelerator-dependent skips are not accelerator acceptance.
MCP tests require the pinned optional SDK: a suite that skips them is not MCP
qualification. Durable-job/MCP tests also need POSIX file locking, signals and real
child processes. See [focused suites and host prerequisites](docs/usage.md#development-tests)
and the [dated MCP compatibility measurements](docs/mcp.md#installation-and-compatibility).
The [CI workflow](.github/workflows/tests.yml) is the executable check definition;
the [recorded environment](docs/installation.md#recorded-environment-and-evidence)
describes previous results, not a fresh run of this checkout.

# Documentation index

Start with three concepts:

1. **Inputs and quantization:** use eligible local model weights and calibration
   text to produce a low-bit checkpoint.
2. **Validation scope:** check structure, dense numerical quality and native
   runtime behavior separately. One does not establish the others.
3. **Execution and recovery:** choose devices, plan resources and keep the source,
   data and work paths needed to resume.

## User journey

| Step | Read |
| --- | --- |
| Start | [Installation and platform boundaries](installation.md), [no-download CPU tutorial](getting-started.md#executed-no-download-cpu-smoke), then [first model and support matrix](getting-started.md) |
| Prepare data | [Separate calibration, training, tuning and final-test inputs](evaluation.md#prepare-independent-data) |
| Quantize | [First-model walkthrough](getting-started.md#first-model-walkthrough), [canonical commands and defaults](usage.md) |
| Validate | [Coverage, size and metric interpretation](evaluation.md#read-the-results), [task benchmark setup and safety](../bench/README.md) |
| Recover | [Phase resources, artifacts and troubleshooting](operations.md) |
| Serve or distribute | [Dense export versus native serving](getting-started.md#export-and-native-serving), [portability](operations.md#distribute-a-checkpoint) |
| Automate locally | [Model Context Protocol (MCP) setup, workspace access and durable jobs](mcp.md) |
| Contribute | [Dependency extras, unit/integration tests and full checks](installation.md#contributor-tests-and-checks) |

Use the walkthrough for explicit recipe settings. Use [usage.md](usage.md) for
command-line options and defaults. Research tables describe the stated runs;
they do not replace current help or guarantee installation compatibility.

<!-- docs/architecture-evaluation-roadmap.md -->
# Architecture, evaluation and research roadmap

Assessment date: **2026-11-05**. Source baseline:
[`8439fca52d`](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/tree/8439fca52d099a39213ab016b8174f663c4eebdf).
Execution order and live status: **[#94](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/94)**.

This report separates implemented behavior from proposed work. It consolidates
five investigations of numerical performance, memory/I/O, control architecture,
allocation/quality and format/runtime boundaries, plus selected CPU reproductions.
The integrated `hi2 bench` command below is **planned, not implemented**.
The tickets are not evidence that their features work.

## Decision

Keep the current numerical pipeline and v3.2 representation. Repair composition,
make measurements complete, and establish native runtime qualification before
adding formats or replacing infrastructure. Existing fusion, batching, prefetch,
activation residency and cache reuse are not missing features.

Use these evidence classes throughout:

- **Observed:** exercised on the baseline during this assessment, on CPU.
- **Source:** supported by code inspection; the consequence may remain untested.
- **Historical:** an earlier documented measurement under its original conditions.
- **Proposal:** an unmeasured benefit or design hypothesis, not a speed promise.

No new GPU performance, native-loader support or model-quality improvement was
established by this assessment. The [current architecture](architecture.md)
explains the quantization phases; [evaluation.md](evaluation.md) defines current
metrics and commands. This report explains what to improve next.

## Current architecture and useful boundaries

The baseline has 38 core Python modules and 15,745 physical source lines,
including embedded native-kernel source strings. Large modules are not by
themselves a reason to split files.

| Boundary | Main modules | Responsibility |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Interfaces | `__main__`, `mcp_server`, `mcp_tasks` | CLI presentation and MCP transport |
| Control | `service`, `workspace`, `jobs`, `job_worker`, `runtime` | Resolution, preflight, access policy, supervision, ownership, identity and results |
| Pipeline | `pipeline`, `layerwise` | Calibration, statistics reuse, sensitivity, allocation, PTQ/QAD and publication |
| Numerical work | `stats`, `hessian`, `calibrate`, `allocate`, `encode`, `refine`, `qad`, `finalkl`, `executor`, kernel modules | Candidate errors, row choices, exact encoding, optional training and device work |
| Storage | `format`, `checkpoint`, `stack`, `cache`, `size`, `moe`, `pinned` | Representation, checkpoint I/O, expert layouts, reusable products and transfers |
| Evaluation | `evalq`, `quality`, `tune`, checkout `bench/` | Dense fidelity, acceptance, target selection and served tasks |

The current pipeline collects activation moments and, for GPTQ, Hessian data;
builds candidate error tables; allocates bits/blocks globally; initializes PTQ
or each QAD layer; optionally performs final-KL training; then writes, restacks
and validates the artifact. The QAD path does not first publish a complete PTQ
checkpoint. Native inference kernels live in an external vLLM fork.

Preserve four separate identities:

- `run_id`: intended work and input identity.
- `attempt_id`: one execution or reuse attempt.
- `job_id`: durable scheduling and supervision.
- Artifact identity: the actual published bytes and their production evidence.

A cache entry is a disposable acceleration product, not proof of artifact
correctness. Static import cycles exist across storage helpers, evaluation/service
helpers and MCP helpers; some are intentional lazy imports. No import failure was
observed. Extract a boundary when a real operation needs it, not through a broad
rename or framework rewrite.

### Architecture direction

Keep typed finite operations in `service`; keep CLI formatting and MCP transport
outside. Preserve `workspace` as the authority for paths and capabilities. A new
adapter must not bypass it.

Artifact transformations must regenerate evidence that depends on changed bytes.
Keep immutable production evidence separate from attempt events. Reuse existing
ownership locks and publication rules rather than introducing another manager.

Distinguish what the encoder can represent, what structural validation accepts,
and what a pinned native runtime has actually executed. Resource plans must state
uncertainty; an estimate is not a memory-fit promise.

## Observations and performance priorities

### Four reproduced contract gaps

| Finding | CPU observation | Ticket |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Reader lifetime | A live `StatsView` loaded a Hessian; another process pruned its entry; the same reader then returned `None`. Missing-Sigma paths can select nearest rounding, but a changed full-run output was not measured. | [#64](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/64) |
| Portable restack | A portable artifact with two experts and six projections validated. Restacking succeeded, but the result failed validation with `config.json: portable checksum/size mismatch`. | [#65](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/65) |
| K alignment | A synthetic, consistently packed INT2/B32 artifact with R=8 and K=32 passed public validation despite the normative K-multiple-of-64 rule. Normal encoder emission of this shape was not demonstrated. | [#66](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/66) |
| Recipe types | Core resolution accepted `schema_version: true` and retained fractional `cache_devices: 0.5`. Other numeric checks already reject booleans; the issue is inconsistent declared-type enforcement. | [#67](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/67) |

These are narrow follow-ups to existing features, not claims that cache locking,
portable export, validation or the shared service are absent.

### CPU execution and timing scope

A no-download random Llama fixture exercised real CLI execution: two layers,
hidden width 128, intermediate width 256, vocabulary 64, 14 quantized linears,
two calibration sequences of 16 tokens, two QAD steps and two final-KL steps.
Math was float32 on CPU, with rotation and sensitivity disabled. This proves
execution paths, not useful language-model quality.

Environment: Python 3.12.13, PyTorch 2.15.0, Transformers 5.22.1, safetensors 0.8.0,
macOS arm64; CPU math thread counts limited to one.

| Case | CLI wall time | Returned numerical total | Interpretation |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| Empty HI2 application cache | 2.815 s | 0.9 s | Statistics miss; successful checkpoint production |
| Warm cache, new run | 2.544 s | 0.6 s | Statistics/tables hit; another successful production run |
| Completed-run reuse | 1.887 s | 0.6 s, original value | Fresh attempt, reused artifact-production metrics |

One observation per case. Empty application cache is not cold OS page cache.
These are not statistically supported speedups or GPU predictions. Current
numerical totals omit preparation/hash and later restack/validation; the QAD
timer includes final-KL. [#68](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/68) separates attempt and production evidence.

### Historical measurements that affect priority

| Workload | Recorded cost | Implication |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Qwen3.5-4B cold PTQ | Phase 1 559 s; probe 825 s; encode 97 s; total about 1,485 s | Phase 1 plus probe were about 93% of that run. Eliminating encoding entirely would cap its whole-run gain at about 1.07x. |
| Qwen3.5-4B layer QAD | Existing fusion reduced a step from 550 to 400 ms; layer forward/backward remained about 376 ms | The old unfused decode cost is not a new optimization opportunity. |
| Completed 27B QAD | Layer QAD 247 min plus final-KL 470.8 min | Final-KL was about 66% of these two training phases, not necessarily of full CLI time. Profile it separately. |

Sources: [PTQ measurements][ptq-cost], [QAD step profile][qad-step],
[27B phase costs][qad-cost]. These are different workloads, not one benchmark
matrix. They justify profiling, not a predicted gain from a particular kernel.

## Original ranked work candidates

The 26 recommendations became 30 atomic tickets. R8, R9, R14 and R20 each contain
two independently deliverable changes. The live roadmap may adjust execution
order as dependencies and measurements become available.

| Rank | Work | Tickets | Main gate |
| ---: | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Active cache-reader leases | [#64](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/64) | CPU multiprocess lifetime and real cache-backed workflow |
| 2 | Portable restack integrity | [#65](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/65) | CPU transformed artifact validation and failure cleanup |
| 3 | Normative K alignment | [#66](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/66) | Public structural validation boundaries |
| 4 | Shared declared-type validation | [#67](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/67) | CLI, recipe, service and MCP agreement |
| 5 | Attempt versus production timing | [#68](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/68) | Cold/warm/reuse accounting; GPU trace for device attribution |
| 6 | Group-at-a-time cached table replay | [#69](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/69) | Identical tables/outputs; GPU memory evidence for claims |
| 7 | One compact calibration preparation | [#70](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/70) | Exact IDs, seed order, lifetimes and identities |
| 8 | Native target selection; build isolation | [#71](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/71), [#72](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/72) | Real architecture-specific and concurrent native builds |
| 9 | Artifact requirements; pinned runtime qualification | [#73](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/73), [#74](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/74) | CPU requirements plus actual native execution |
| 10 | Tuning provenance and final-test exclusion | [#75](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/75) | End-to-end data lineage and overlap rejection |
| 11 | Confirm selected target-KLD point | [#76](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/76) | Measured versus predicted quality and bounded search |
| 12 | Token/context identity for score gaps | [#77](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/77) | Correct pairing and real endpoint records |
| 13 | Reuse contiguous shared Hessian factors | [#78](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/78) | Exact outputs and measured GPU allocations |
| 14 | Cache final-KL transform flags; batch metric transfers | [#79](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/79), [#80](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/80) | Same numerical decisions and actual synchronization evidence |
| 15 | Inner final-KL checkpoint tradeoff | [#81](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/81) | GPU recomputation/memory study, adopt or reject |
| 16 | Fixed-code final-KL decoder | [#82](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/82) | Profile first; exact forward, correct gradients and real training |
| 17 | Byte-bounded pending Sigma writes | [#83](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/83) | Queue lifetime/failure behavior and actual high-water |
| 18 | Aggregate pinned staging bound | [#84](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/84) | Real mixed-shape/dtype/thread GPU transfers |
| 19 | Sharing-aware device admission | [#85](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/85) | Output invariants, conservative peaks and native timings |
| 20 | Shared tuning operation; durable tuning jobs | [#86](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/86), [#87](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/87) | Real service/CLI then reconnectable MCP lifecycle |
| 21 | Per-operation capability states | [#88](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/88) | Separate installed, eligible, executable and qualified |
| 22 | Allocation tradeoff explanation | [#89](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/89) | Auditable objective/constraint/byte accounting |
| 23 | Reachable QAD stopping checks | [#90](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/90) | Schedule transitions plus real quality/work tradeoff |
| 24 | Predeclared regression policy | [#91](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/91) | Accepted, rejected and insufficient-evidence distinctions |
| 25 | Numerical producer identity audit | [#92](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/92) | Equivalence evidence or compatible cache invalidation |
| 26 | Queue contention study | [#93](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/93) | CPU multiprocess measurements before scheduler changes |

Existing [#39](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/39) owns the native serving guide and [#53](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/53) owns broader model
qualification. Neither was replaced with a duplicate ticket.

## Proposed integrated evaluation command

The proposed entry point is **`hi2 bench`** ([#101](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/101)), over a shared service operation.
Keep **`hi2 quality`** as the focused numerical-fidelity command. Reuse its
evaluator and thresholds; do not create a second KL/PPL implementation.

Today, `hi2 validate`, `verify`, `size`, `eval` and `quality` are available.
Task runs use checkout scripts `bench/run.py` and `bench/compare.py`; `bench/`
is not installed by package discovery. Dense evaluation reconstructs fp16 weights
and does not test native HI2 kernels. The current [benchmark guide](../bench/README.md)
describes commands, server API requirements and execution risks.

The following diagram is the **planned workflow**, not current command behavior:

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    Entry[CLI or durable MCP evaluation suite] --> Plan[Versioned plan and identities]
    Plan --> Checks[Artifact validation and independent-data checks]
    Checks --> Runs[Matched runs and reference repeats]
    Runs --> Fidelity[Existing quality and size operations]
    Runs --> Utility[Paired task evaluation with isolated code execution]
    Runs --> Native[Native performance and stability adapters]
    Fidelity --> Evidence[Raw results, provenance and coverage]
    Utility --> Evidence
    Native --> Evidence
    Evidence --> Policy[Predeclared acceptance policy]
    Policy --> Result[Accepted, rejected or insufficient evidence]
```

### What a good-quant decision must measure

| Dimension | Required evidence | Existing work and missing tickets |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Fidelity | Held-out full-distribution KL, PPL and PPL ratio against the matching source; top-1 agreement and distribution overlap; actual serialized size | Existing `quality`/`size`, orchestrated by [#101](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/101) |
| Utility | Same math, coding, instruction, tool-use and long-context items; per-task outcomes and paired uncertainty | Installed runner [#96](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/96); isolated code [#97](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/97); optional standard-task adapter [#103](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/103) and EvalPlus [#104](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/104) |
| Deployment | Actual native load/execution, peak memory, prefill/decode and aggregate throughput, TTFT/inter-token latency, errors and repeated-request stability | Qualification [#74](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/74); performance [#98](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/98); stability [#99](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/99) |
| Fair comparison | Matching input items, tokenizer/template, thinking/decoding settings and context budget; pinned model/artifact/task identity; disclosed runtime/dtype differences | Shared plan [#95](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/95); token-score pairing [#77](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/77); cross-GGUF study [#105](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/105) |
| Independent evidence | Final-test data outside calibration and tuning; predeclared policy; repeated reference runs to estimate runtime noise | Provenance [#75](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/75); repeat protocol [#100](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/100); acceptance [#91](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/91) |

Completion and acceptance are different. A wrong answer is a completed item;
a request failure is missing evidence. A full-suite result cannot be accepted
when a required phase is skipped, incompatible or unmeasured. An explicit
CPU-only selection can complete, but must state that native evidence is absent.
There is no universal KL or task-score threshold that certifies every use case.

A selected-token log-probability gap is not full-distribution KL. Expected
acceptance from distribution overlap is not measured speculative-serving
acceptance. Client wall time is not native-kernel time. Reference run-to-run
spread is different from paired item-bootstrap uncertainty; report both.

### Delivery boundaries

- [#95](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/95) defines the plan and comparison identity before expensive execution.
- [#96](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/96) makes the existing runner/resources usable outside a checkout.
- [#97](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/97) provides real isolation. The current `bench/sandbox.py` is not a security
  sandbox; do not silently run generated code on the host.
- [#98](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/98) and [#99](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/99) require actual native runtimes. CPU protocol tests cannot qualify
  GPU memory, speed or graph replay.
- [#100](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/100) records repeats/restarts/cache state without treating greedy decoding as
  a determinism guarantee.
- [#101](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/101) composes the measured stages and existing policy; no scaffold-only success.
- [#102](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/102) exposes that same operation as reconnectable MCP jobs, preserving existing
  idempotency, output/device leases and workspace capabilities.
- [#106](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/106) maintains a small, evidence-backed dependency matrix and reproducible
  environments, rather than blindly pinning all dependencies.

The optional integrations are research: [lm-evaluation-harness][lm-eval] ([#103](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/103)),
[EvalPlus][evalplus] ([#104](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/104)) and [llama.cpp GGUF evaluation][llama-ppl] ([#105](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/105)).
Pin versions and task protocols. The local generated `ifeval` task is not an
official IFEval result. GGUF tools do not provide an HI2 loader. Keep numerical
cross-format comparisons separate from native speed and memory comparisons.

## Remaining format and algorithm explorations

These tickets request measurements and an adopt/reject decision. They do not
change defaults or authorize an unversioned extension to v3.2.

### B128: fewer block parameters, potentially more quantization error

B128 means 128 weights share one affine ladder along K, compared with 64 for
B64. HI2 stores two parameter bytes per block, so the modeled parameter cost
is `16 / B` bits per weight.

| Block | Parameter overhead | INT2 codes plus block parameters |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| B64, implemented | 0.250 bpw | 2.250 bpw |
| B128, proposed | 0.125 bpw | 2.125 bpw |

This excludes row constants, offsets, smoothing, dense tensors and other
serialized metadata. It is not whole-checkpoint bpw. B128 does not reduce the
number of INT2 code bits; it reduces the frequency of ladder parameters.

The larger block must fit more weights with the same four INT2 reconstruction
levels. Error can increase, and the allocator may compensate with higher-bit
rows, removing the size benefit. Native decoding may save parameter traffic,
but arithmetic/dispatch constraints can dominate. K divisibility, GPTQ/QAD
handling, validation and external loader/kernels need explicit support.

**Study [#107](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/107):** first compare actual size and independent quality against current
mixed blocks/rows. Proceed to native prototypes only if that frontier is useful.
Do not silently add B128 to the v3.2 contract. See [normative storage][format].

### Wider rotations: not an assumed improvement

Current rotation groups have width 16. Wider groups may spread outliers across
more columns, but add butterfly stages and activation-side costs. They must
preserve the paired weight/activation basis, shared-input transforms and block
alignment. They are not free preprocessing when activations also need rotation.

Historical small-model measurements found groups 16/64/128 within about 1%
after smoothing ([design notes](design-notes.md#2-where-rotation-helps-and-where-it-does-not)).
That limits the claim; it is not a broad proof of either equivalence or benefit.
**Study [#108](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/108)** needs a new workload-specific reason, independent quality and
native timings, with versioned semantics if the representation changes.

### Outlier side channel: residual values, not a security mechanism

An outlier side channel stores selected high-precision corrections beside the
low-bit base weights. The model reconstructs the base plus those corrections.
Indices, residual values, gathers and correction work all count toward cost.

**Study [#109](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/109)** compares this with the existing INT2/INT4/optional INT8 row mix,
not with uniform INT2 alone. Require equal-size/equal-quality comparisons and
native dense/MoE measurements. Reject it if metadata or runtime overhead erases
the gain. A new residual representation needs explicit decode/version rules.

### Latency-aware allocation: measured costs before a new objective

The current objective is weighted error versus modeled bits, not serving latency.
Per-row latency is not necessarily additive. Mixed kernels, input shapes, expert
routing, prefill/decode, concurrency and activation mode affect real cost.

**Study [#110](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/110)** starts from [#98](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/98) native measurements and [#73](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/73)/#74 qualified runtime
requirements. Validate a cost model on held-out shapes/workloads and compare
quality, actual size and latency together. A microbenchmark win that worsens the
full serving workload is not an improvement. [#113](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/113) separately studies filtering
existing candidates to a qualified runtime profile; capability filtering is not
itself latency optimization.

### INT3: low-priority feasibility only

INT3 is not assumed relevant to the performance target. Existing INT2/INT4 mixing
already provides intermediate average precision. Three-bit packing requires a
new extraction contract, including word-boundary behavior, rather than the
current power-of-two packing. Smaller nominal codes do not establish faster
decode on RDNA2.

**Study [#111](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/111) is P3:** screen the quality/actual-size opportunity first. Only
consider native implementation if the expected benefit can survive packing,
metadata and decode costs. An evidence-backed rejection is a valid outcome.

### Other candidates and explicit limits

| Study | Question | Gate |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [#112](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/112) nonlinear/vector/trellis codebooks | Is there enough equal-size quality gain to justify a more complex decode path? | New semantics, all metadata bytes, real native cost and independent quality |
| [#113](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/113) runtime-constrained candidates | Does filtering existing choices to a qualified runtime help? | Profile identity, feasible choices and changed relative error floor; no assumed speedup |
| [#114](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/114) final-KL token sampling | Does the existing `final_tokens` option save worthwhile head/loss work? | Decoder work is not removed; measure final quality and whole-run cost |
| [#115](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/115) selective QAD precision | Can a narrower mixed-precision branch avoid the known full-FP16 failure? | Numerical explanation, complete GPU training and held-out quality; do not rerun the known failure merely to confirm it |
| [#116](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/116) FP16 Hessian policy | Where is the existing lossy cache option a useful tradeoff? | Cache/host/device cost, changed codes and model quality; no exactness claim |
| [#117](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/117) direct stacked publication | Is staging/restack I/O material enough to justify a writer change? | Profile first, then artifact equivalence, ownership and interruption safety |

[Full-FP16 QAD history][fp16-qad] records poor learning and dtype failures.
[FP16 Hessian history][fp16-sigma] records changed codes. These are constraints,
not default settings to promote from theoretical throughput.

Already-slower broad phase-1 table batching, removal of safe staging without
transfer evidence, mtime-only model identities, unbounded global factor caches
and a distributed scheduler/framework rewrite are **not** implementation backlog.
The queue study [#93](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/93) addresses observed contention if it becomes material; it
does not authorize a new scheduler by assumption.

## Validation and next order

1. Fix the four reproduced contract gaps.
2. Establish timing, comparison identities and independent-data lineage.
3. Build evaluation plan/packaging/isolation and unblocked CPU improvements.
4. Qualify native builds/runtime and complete native evaluation stages.
5. Select GPU optimizations using profiles; run bounded format/training studies
   only when their prerequisites and acceptance criteria justify them.

Independent CPU work need not wait for GPU access. Each change needs relevant
consumer-visible regressions, a real changed-path smoke and updated documentation.
GPU-dependent changes stay draft/unqualified until the required evidence exists.
For performance claims, pin model/runtime/config/device, state cache warmth,
measure whole-run and phase costs plus peaks, repeat runs and verify correct
output. Training/policy changes also need untouched final data and utility tests.

The live checklist in [#94](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/94) tracks all 53 new tickets plus existing [#39](https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/issues/39)/#53.
Update tickets with actual results; revise priorities after measurement rather
than automatically implement every proposal.

[ptq-cost]: https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/blob/8439fca52d099a39213ab016b8174f663c4eebdf/docs/speed.md#L169-L220
[qad-step]: https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/blob/8439fca52d099a39213ab016b8174f663c4eebdf/docs/qad.md#L289-L312
[qad-cost]: https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/blob/8439fca52d099a39213ab016b8174f663c4eebdf/docs/qad.md#L34-L46
[format]: https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/blob/8439fca52d099a39213ab016b8174f663c4eebdf/docs/format-spec.md#L40-L123
[fp16-qad]: https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/blob/8439fca52d099a39213ab016b8174f663c4eebdf/docs/qad.md#L643-L695
[fp16-sigma]: https://github.com/BlivionIaG/hi2/blob/8439fca52d099a39213ab016b8174f663c4eebdf/docs/speed.md#L278-L283
[lm-eval]: https://github.com/EleutherAI/lm-evaluation-harness
[evalplus]: https://github.com/evalplus/evalplus
[llama-ppl]: https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/blob/master/tools/perplexity/README.md


<!-- docs/architecture.md -->
# How hi2 works

`hi2` has four conceptual phases. Phases 1 and 2 select the allocation.
Post-training quantization (**PTQ**) then encodes the weights.
With quantization-aware distillation (**QAD**) enabled, PTQ initializes each
layer before training. The final streaming writer publishes the trained
tensors afterwards; it does not first publish a complete PTQ checkpoint.
PTQ-only runs skip distillation.

For the dated assessment and proposed work, see the
[architecture and evaluation roadmap](architecture-evaluation-roadmap.md).
Its evaluation command and format experiments are proposals, not current behavior.

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    M[HF checkpoint] --> P1
    T[calibration text] --> P1
    subgraph P1[1 Tabulate - layerwise.py, stats.py, calibrate.py]
        S[one decoder layer at a time:<br/>16x16 second-moment blocks per column group]
        E[error table per row:<br/>every bits x block option]
        S --> E
    end
    P1 --> P2
    subgraph P2[2 Allocate - allocate.py, sensitivity.py]
        A[pick bits and block for every row<br/>under one model-wide error or bit budget]
    end
    P2 --> P3
    subgraph P3[3 Encode - encode.py, hessian.py]
        G[GPTQ rounding with fixed ladders<br/>per-layer initialization when QAD is on]
    end
    P3 -->|QAD enabled| P4
    subgraph P4[4 Distill - qad.py, finalkl.py]
        Q[train latent weights and ladder bytes<br/>against the unquantized layer output]
        K[final logit-KL training<br/>default/thorough request it; fit check can skip]
        Q --> K
    end
    P4 --> W[checkpoint.py: publish tensors and metadata]
    P3 -->|PTQ only| W
    W --> C[rdna_hint_flex checkpoint]
```

## Phase 1: tabulate

The tool copies one decoder layer to a compute device. It runs the calibration sequences
through that layer. Hooks collect, for every Linear, a 16x16 second-moment block for each
column group. For input width `K`, this uses `K * 16` floats per tensor.
These blocks let the tool compute each row's activation-weighted error in the
rotated basis without keeping the activations.

Each Linear gets a table: the weighted squared error of every row under every candidate
(INT2 or INT4, block 16, 32 or 64). INT8 candidates exist and are off by default.

## Phase 2: allocate

Every row of the whole model chooses its candidate. The tool minimizes bits under one
model-wide error limit (`--max-error-ratio`), or minimizes error under a bit budget
(`--target-bpw`). A sensitivity probe gives each tensor a weight, so errors in sensitive
tensors cost more. The result is a mix: INT2 for rows that matter less, INT4 for the rest.

The quality constraint is global: the summed sensitivity-weighted candidate
errors must fit R times the summed best-candidate floor. A row may exceed R
times its own minimum; the sum, not each row, is constrained. This dimensionless
allocation proxy is not a bound on end-to-end Kullback–Leibler divergence (KLD)
([evaluation.md](evaluation.md#allocation-and-kld-targets)).

## Phase 3: encode

The tool streams the checkpoint again. It encodes each tensor with its picks. Each block
stores `w = fp16(lo + code * step)`. Per-row fp16 constants scale 8-bit `lo` and `step`
bytes. GPTQ rounding picks the codes with the Hessian and corrects the error as it moves
along the columns. The ladders stay fixed, so rounding changes only which codes win.

Tensors are written as `<name>.hi2_*`. Other tensors are copied.

## Phase 4: distill (QAD)

QAD trains one layer at a time. The student layer is a fake-quantized copy that follows the
exact decode of the format. Its input is the hidden state of the already quantized prefix.
Its target is the unquantized teacher layer's output on the teacher prefix.
A straight-through estimator (STE) passes gradients through rounding.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    X[hidden state from<br/>quantized prefix] --> S[student layer<br/>FakeQuantLinear]
    X2[hidden state from<br/>teacher prefix] --> T[teacher layer]
    S --> L[mean-squared error on layer output]
    T --> L
    L -->|STE gradients| S
    S -->|keep if it beats PTQ<br/>on internal validation| O[export bit-exact]
```

Row picks, smoothing vectors, and rotation flags stay as PTQ chose them.
Keep-best selects among evaluated local states, including PTQ. It does not
guarantee final-corpus KLD or task accuracy.

The final logit-KL **training** phase (`--qad-final-kl`) trains only ladder bytes
and, by default, norms; codes stay fixed. The `default` and `thorough` presets
request 200 steps. The `fast` preset requests none, and an explicit 0 disables it.
The phase shards the student across devices and uses saved teacher head inputs
when available. A failed model-fit estimate skips it with a note.
Skipped training is not completed training, and completed training is not
independent final quality evaluation. Use `quality` on separate final-test text
to measure and accept or reject the published artifact.

## Module map

| Module | Job |
| --- | --- |
| `__main__.py` | CLI adapters and command presentation. |
| `service.py`, `workspace.py` | Shared resolution and finite operations; rooted paths and operator-owned capabilities. |
| `runtime.py`, `jobs.py`, `job_worker.py` | Run/attempt identity, ownership, durable job state and process supervision. |
| `mcp_server.py`, `mcp_tasks.py` | MCP transport and negotiated task projection over the existing jobs. |
| `pipeline.py` | The phases, `RunConfig`, resume logic. |
| `format.py` | The storage format, decode, and reference semantics. |
| `encode.py` | Block encoder: least-squares fit of `lo` and `step`, 8-bit parameters, code assignment. |
| `calibrate.py` | Candidate tables per tensor. |
| `layerwise.py`, `stats.py` | Layer-by-layer statistics collection. |
| `hessian.py`, `gptq_kernel.py` | Hessian-aware rounding. The kernel is a fused HIP loop for gfx10 and gfx11. |
| `allocate.py`, `sensitivity.py` | Global allocation and the sensitivity probe. |
| `qad.py`, `finalkl.py` | Layer-wise distillation and the final logit-KL phase. |
| `moe.py`, `stack.py` | Expert views, routed-token statistics, the stacked layout. |
| `checkpoint.py` | Shard enumeration, the streaming writer, verification. |
| `inkling_source.py` | Optional source reader of the stock Inkling-2-Mini NVFP4/MXFP8 checkpoint and its decoded view for `checkpoint.source_map` / `open_source` ([inkling-source.md](inkling-source.md)); reached only for a directory of that layout. |
| `cache.py` | The persistent cache. |
| `executor.py` | Compute placement on one or more devices. |
| `evalq.py`, `quality.py` | Dense held-out fidelity and explicit acceptance thresholds. |
| `tune.py` | Target-KLD search; predictions are distinct from measured final quality. |
| `size.py` | Byte accounting of a checkpoint (`hi2 size`). |
| `calib.py` | Mixed-domain calibration text (`hi2 calib`). |

## What the cache holds

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    K[model + calibration ids + settings] --> H{key}
    H --> A[statistics]
    H --> B[Hessians]
    H --> C[error tables]
    H --> D[probe results]
```

The **cache** stores reusable phase products. The **workdir** stores one run's
identity and recovery state. The **output** stores the published checkpoint and
reports. Workdir and output reuse require a matching `run_id`; each execution
gets a fresh `attempt_id`. A durable submission has a separate `job_id`.
See [identity fields and paths](usage.md#cache-and-work-directory).

Statistics keys include sampled token IDs, the `sha256-content-v1` model
fingerprint and relevant settings. Compatible Hessians can survive allocation
and encoder changes. The model fingerprint hashes full config/index/shard
contents and relative filenames. Statistics version 2 invalidated the earlier
names-and-sizes-only identity; current `STATS_VERSION=3` also includes active
auxiliary model inputs by canonical path and full SHA-256.
Hashing costs a checkpoint read and does not permit changing inputs during
execution. See [speed.md](speed.md#the-cache) and
[recovery rules](operations.md#resume-and-invalidation).

## Boundaries

* The tool writes checkpoints. The vLLM fork only reads them. The contract between them is
  the tensor layout in [format-spec.md](format-spec.md).
* Calibration and QAD live only in this tool. The runtime does not contain them.
* The tool changes no model code. `eval` runs the original architecture on dequantized
  weights.


<!-- docs/design-notes.md -->
# HI2 design notes: rotation, and where it stands against GGUF and EXL3

Companion to `format-spec.md` (the format) and `./README.md` (the
quantizer). Everything measured here is Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct on held-out text, one
run each, so differences under about 5% are noise. Raw data is in
`experiments/results/`.

## 1. What HI2 is

A weight format for RDNA2 (`gfx1030`): per row INT2 or INT4 codes, blocks of 16, 32
or 64 along the input dimension, an affine ladder `lo + code * step` whose two
parameters are stored as 8 bits relative to three fp16 per-row constants, an optional
per-column smoothing vector, and an optional order-16 Hadamard rotation of the input
dimension. Decode is fixed and branch-free, aimed at `v_dot2_f32_f16` (fp16
activations) or `v_dot4_i32_i8` (int8 activations). Calibration (activation
statistics, encoder, global bits-per-weight allocation) lives in the separate
`hi2` quantizer, not in vLLM.

## 2. Where rotation helps, and where it does not

A rotation spreads a few large weights over a group, which is good for a quantizer
whose step size is set by the block range. It also mixes columns, which is bad for a
quantizer that treats columns differently. What we measured:

| Encoder | rotation off | rotation on |
| --- | --- | --- |
| data-free (no activation statistics), 3.0 bpw | 44.28 ppl | **30.71 ppl** (-31%) |
| calibrated (activation-weighted), 3.0 bpw | **19.92 ppl** | 30.50 ppl (+53%) |
| calibrated, smoothing 0.5 first, 3.0 bpw | n/a | **18.31 ppl** (-8% vs the best unrotated) |

So:

* **Data-free: always rotate.** This is the case the literature (QuIP#, QuaRot,
  EXL3) describes, and it holds here.
* **Calibrated: the encoder already uses activation scale per channel**, similar in
  effect to AWQ. Rotating on top of that destroys what it exploits, so rotation
  alone is worse.
* **Calibrated with smoothing first: best.** Dividing each activation channel by
  `s_j = (E[x_j^2] / geomean)^0.5` (and multiplying the weight column by `s_j`) makes
  the channels comparable; the rotation then only has weight outliers left to flatten.
  This helped at every bit width: perplexity -30% at 2.5 bpw, -8% at 3.0, -5% at 3.5,
  -1% at 4.0, -1% at 4.5 (KL -39% at 4.5). Smoothing strength matters: 0.25 is worse
  than none, 0.5 and 0.75 are equivalent.
* **Group size and signs do not matter much.** After smoothing, groups of 16, 64 and
  128 are within 1%. Random signs (QuIP#-style) made no difference. Group 16 stays,
  because the activation transform is then four butterfly stages and a block never
  splits a group.

Runtime cost of the smoothing and rotation: one divide by `s` plus a 16-point
Walsh-Hadamard per token per layer, fused with the activation quantization in int8
mode. The smoothing vector costs `16/R` bpw per tensor (0.125 bpw for a 128-row
projection in this model, 0.004 at R=4096). q, k and v share an input and so share
`s`; gate and up likewise. Storing it once, or folding it into the preceding RMSNorm
weight (free at runtime), is the obvious next saving and is not implemented. `o_proj`
and `down_proj` inputs have no norm to fold into.

Not tried, and what the literature does that we do not: a full-dimension Hadamard
(EXL3, QuIP#) rather than 16 columns, rotating the residual stream once so most of
the rotations cost nothing at runtime (QuaRot, SpinQuant), and Hessian-aware rounding
(see 4).

## 3. Measured against GGUF and EXL3

Primary comparison: **Qwen3.5-0.8B** (hybrid linear/full attention, hidden 1024, MLP
3584, both multiples of 256 so GGUF needs no fallbacks), one GPU (gfx1030), same
held-out text for everything (8 x 2048 tokens at offset 800000 of the repo
documentation). The EXL3 checkpoint is the one already on the box
(`Qwen3.5-0.8B-exl3-3inst-good`, 3.0 bpw, 3inst codebook, 6-bit head, calibrated on
256 x 2048). Everything below is **perplexity divided by the same model's
unquantized perplexity**. A ratio of 1.00 means equal measured perplexity, not
identical distributions, generations or task accuracy. Cross-evaluator windows
remain a caveat; raw data is in `experiments/results/e2e_qwen3.5-0.8b.json`.

Evaluator check: vLLM (fork, fp16) scores the unquantized model 10.8736 and the HI2
export 20.8727; my HF evaluator scores them 10.8738 and 20.8734. EXL3 and HI2 were
scored in the same vLLM, the GGUF types and HI2 in the same `llama-perplexity`.

| Format | bits per weight (linear layers) | perplexity / unquantized |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **EXL3 3inst** | 3.0 (nominal) | **1.12** (vLLM) |
| HI2, Hessian-aware rounding | 2.5 | 1.53 (HF), 1.57 (llama.cpp) |
| HI2, Hessian-aware rounding | 3.0 | 1.32 (HF), 1.32 (vLLM), 1.33 (llama.cpp) |
| HI2, Hessian-aware rounding | 3.5 | 1.18 (HF), 1.20 (llama.cpp) |
| HI2, Hessian-aware rounding | 4.0 | 1.07 (HF), 1.07 (llama.cpp) |
| HI2, Hessian-aware rounding | 4.5 | 1.02 (HF), 1.02 (llama.cpp) |
| HI2, nearest rounding | 3.0 | 1.92 |
| HI2, nearest rounding | 4.0 | 1.19 |
| GGUF Q2_K | 3.26 | 1.74 |
| GGUF Q3_K_S | 3.46 | 1.37 |
| GGUF Q3_K_M | 3.96 | 1.27 |
| GGUF IQ4_XS | 4.57 | 1.044 |
| GGUF Q4_K_M | 4.97 | 1.042 |
| GGUF Q5_K_M | 5.76 | 1.012 |

GGUF's i-quants (IQ2 and IQ3) fail to quantize this model, and the ROCmFPX
`Q2_0_ROCMFPX` (2.5 bpw) scores a perplexity of 681, so neither is a data point.

What this says:

* **Hessian-aware rounding closed most of the gap to EXL3** (spec 8.3). At 3.0 bpw HI2
  went from 1.92 to 1.32, against EXL3's 1.12. HI2 reaches 1.12 at about 3.76 bpw, so
  EXL3 is now about 0.75 bpw (20%) smaller at equal quality, down from 1.2 bpw.
* **HI2 now beats GGUF's k-quants clearly**, in the same llama.cpp evaluator and
  interpolated to GGUF's bit widths: about 27% better than Q2_K, 12% better than
  Q3_K_S, 15% better than Q3_K_M, and slightly better than IQ4_XS and Q4_K_M with
  fewer bits.
* **Cost:** quantization takes about 3.7x longer than nearest rounding (367 s against
  99 s for this model on one gfx1030 GPU) and needs the full Hessians (about 2 GB of
  scratch for this model). It changes nothing at inference.
* Qwen2.5-0.5B (hidden 896) behaves the same way: 1.75 to 1.32 at 3.0 bpw and 1.15 to
  1.08 at 4.0 bpw. It cannot be used for the GGUF low-bit comparison, because its
  dimensions are not multiples of 256 and GGUF falls back to legacy types.

Caveats: one small hybrid model, one text, one run each. EXL3's "3.0" is its nominal
bit width and the checkpoint also carries per-tensor sign vectors and a 6-bit head;
HI2's bpw includes everything in the linear layers but keeps the embedding and head
dense fp16, so compare body quality, not file size. EXL3 calibrated on 524k tokens;
the HI2 runs above used 65k (524k gave 1% more). Speed and VRAM are not compared.

## 4. What is left between HI2 and EXL3

EXL3 (QTIP) is supported on this fork on RDNA2: native ROCm kernels
(`csrc/rocm/exl3_dot2_*.cu`, `exl3_hadamard.cu`: trellis GEMM, the 3inst and mul1
codebooks, the 128-wide Hadamard). (An earlier version of these notes called EXL3
CUDA-only; that was wrong.) The remaining gap in 3 (1.32 against 1.12 at 3.0 bpw) is
probably, in order of what I can say about it:

* **Codebook.** EXL3 uses a trellis codebook, which gets much closer to the
  rate-distortion bound at 2-3 bits than a uniform scalar ladder. Closing that needs a
  new code type and decode path, not a quantizer change, so it is out of scope for the
  current format. It is the part of the gap HI2's approach cannot recover.
* **Full-dimension rotation.** EXL3 rotates across 128-wide blocks over the matrix; HI2
  mixes 16 columns. Under compensated rounding, rotation matters less (the transform
  cells span 1.32-1.39), so this is probably a small part.
* **Joint ladder and rounding optimisation.** The PTQ encoder fits ladders before
  GPTQ rounding. Later QAD can train ladder bytes and codes, and local PTQ code
  refinement is implemented; a separate PTQ ladder refit after rounding remains
  a distinct proposal ([format-spec.md](format-spec.md), sections 8.9 and 13).

HI2 targets a decode path with a few fixed RDNA2 dot-product instructions rather
than a codebook lookup or trellis walk, input widths divisible by 64 and mixed
INT2/INT4/opt-in INT8 rows. The original PTQ study ran in minutes on one V620;
that is not a time estimate for default QAD plus final-KL. Native HI2 kernels
and loader measurements now exist in the external fork ([results.md](results.md)).
The earlier “no kernel/speed unmeasured” status is superseded, without implying
every model/layout/runtime revision is qualified.

## 5. Research directions and subsequent implementation status

The original priorities below motivated later work; distinguish that completed
work from proposals rather than treating this list as current operating guidance.

1. **Implemented: Hessian-aware rounding** (spec 8.3), the largest single gain
   after calibration in the initial study. Sequential propagation, more
   calibration tokens and a different smoothing exponent did not help there.
2. **Larger-model measurements now exist.** [results.md](results.md) includes 4B,
   OLMoE and 27B runs. Coverage is still finite; Hessian memory scales as `K^2`
   per distinct input, and the [phase resource plan](operations.md) still matters.
3. **Sensitivity-aware allocation is implemented** (spec 8.6). It did not make
   flex beat uniform INT4/B64 at 4.3 bpw in the original study.
4. **Local code refinement is implemented** (spec 8.9). A separate ladder refit
   after PTQ rounding is a proposal, not an enabled encoder mode.
5. **Shared transforms are implemented** for shared inputs; storing their
   smoothing vector once or folding it into RMSNorm remains distinct work.
6. **Native kernels/loader have measured results** in the external fork.
   Reproducible runtime qualification remains a [separate handoff](getting-started.md#export-and-native-serving).
7. **A trellis or vector code type**, if the remaining 0.75 bpw against EXL3 matters
   enough to change the format.


<!-- docs/evaluation.md -->
# Prepare data and read the results

Use `validate` for structural validity, `verify` for sampled weight comparisons,
and `size` for byte accounting. Use `eval` to measure dense numerical quality,
or `quality` to measure it and apply explicit acceptance thresholds. The
[benchmark harness](../bench/README.md) measures named tasks. None of these
checks replaces the others; dense evaluation does not test native HI2 kernels.

**PTQ** means post-training quantization. **QAD** means quantization-aware
distillation. **KLD** means Kullback–Leibler divergence between the reference
and quantized token distributions. The final logit-KL phase is **training**;
“final test” below means independent evaluation after training and selection.
**bpw** means bits per weight.

## Prepare independent data

Assign document identities to these roles **before** mixing or tokenizing:

| Role | Use | Boundary |
| --- | --- | --- |
| PTQ calibration | Statistics, Hessians, candidate errors and sensitivity probing | PTQ takes a prefix of the calibration input. This is training/selection data, not a test set. |
| QAD training and internal validation | Seeded windows from the entire calibration input; internal validation chooses retained states | Internal validation is used by training and is not untouched final evaluation. It can share the PTQ prefix. |
| Tuning validation | `--eval-text` for KLD-target search, or repeated comparison of ratios/recipes | Once used to choose a checkpoint, it is selection data. Do not report it as an untouched final test. |
| Final test | One independent evaluation after choosing the recipe/checkpoint | Reserve a nonempty corpus outside **all** calibration, QAD and tuning inputs. Do not tune on its scores. |

PTQ and QAD may use the same calibration file. QAD separates its own training
and internal-validation windows, but uses both to produce the model.
Reserve tuning and final-test documents separately. They should represent the
target domain without sharing document identities with training inputs.
This guidance does not establish leakage in every historical experiment.
Retain each result's actual protocol and limitations.

### Token budgets and offsets

These are the current standard-recipe requirements, not additional independent
CLI defaults. [usage.md](usage.md) and command help remain the options reference.

| Phase/recipe | Token requirement at sequence length 2048 |
| --- | ---: |
| PTQ, 128 calibration sequences | 128 x 2048 = 262,144 |
| QAD `fast` or `default`, 256 training + 8 internal validation windows | 264 x 2048 = 540,672 |
| QAD `thorough`, 512 + 8 windows | 520 x 2048 = 1,064,960 |
| Default standalone `eval`, 8 sequences | 8 x 2048 = 16,384 usable input tokens after any offset |

For calibrated QAD, the input must satisfy **both** PTQ and QAD requirements,
not their sum: windows can overlap the PTQ prefix. With balanced multi-file QAD
sampling, each input needs enough full windows for its assigned share; a large
aggregate alone is insufficient. Source: [`pipeline.py`](../hi2/pipeline.py)
(`load_calibration_ids`, `sample_calibration_windows`) and
[`QadConfig`/presets](../hi2/qad.py).

Use offset zero for a separately held-out file:

```bash
hi2 eval MODEL OUT --text final-test.txt --skip-tokens 0 --device cpu
```

Here `MODEL` and `OUT` are the matching local source and completed HI2 checkpoint;
the final-test file must contain at least 16,384 model-tokenizer tokens.
At these settings the scorer evaluates 8 x 2047 = 16,376 next-token positions
(the last position in each window has no target).

An offset of 800,000 is meaningful only for an explicitly larger corpus with at
least 816,384 tokens, where the evaluated documents are independently excluded
from every training/selection input. It is **not** suitable for a dedicated
200,000-token holdout, and skipping the PTQ prefix in a QAD calibration file
never establishes that isolation: QAD samples across the whole file.

### Safe preparation and exact overlap

1. Keep source documents and their provenance. Reserve final-test documents
   first, then tuning documents; never pass either source set to calibration.
2. Normalize records the same way you will feed the model. For an exact-duplicate
   check, hash normalized document text (for example, SHA-256 of UTF-8 text after
   a fixed whitespace normalization). Group equal hashes **globally across all
   sources**, not separately by file, and assign each group to one role only.
3. Check set intersections: calibration/QAD versus tuning, calibration/QAD versus
   final, and tuning versus final must all be empty. Save document hashes and
   split assignments so an overlap decision remains auditable after interleaving.
   Check chunked documents by original document identity as well as text hash;
   different chunks of one document are not independent documents.
4. Mix the calibration documents through the whole output rather than placing
   one domain after another. Run the source model's tokenizer and inspect actual
   token counts, not a character estimate or requested target alone.
5. Check the tuning and final files are nonempty and have enough usable tokens.
   Whole-document selection can overshoot a token target; small or exhausted
   sources can miss it. A successful file write alone is not split validation.

The current `hi2 calib --holdout` implementation assigns exact **prepared-text**
identities globally across its supplied sources, with deterministic SHA-256
ordering. Duplicate occurrences remain in one role even when repeated in other
sources; occurrences within that role are retained, not all removed. A requested
holdout needs at least two unique documents, retains at least one calibration
identity, and rejects an empty/tokenless or below-minimum holdout. For the
standard evaluation budget, request `--min-holdout-tokens 16384` with
`--tokenizer MODEL`; character estimates cannot establish a model-token minimum.
Inspect actual calibration/holdout counts, duplicate counts and realized token
shares in the report ([`calib.py`](../hi2/calib.py)).

This is an implemented correction to the earlier positional splitter, which
could produce empty holdouts or exact overlap across duplicate sources.
It isolates only identities in that invocation, not separate mixer invocations
or externally prepared files. Its equality rule is exact rendered text, not the
stronger optional whitespace/provenance normalization suggested above.
It cannot detect paraphrases, near-duplicates or different chunks of one original
document. Preserve the global split checks across your four roles.

An automatically produced holdout becomes **tuning** data if used to choose a
ratio. Keep the independent final corpus outside that invocation. Different
filenames, positional offsets and distinct JSON records alone are not evidence
of independence.

### Input formats and chat

* `calib` reads plain-text blocks and JSON/JSONL text or chat records (`messages`
  or `conversations`). Its normal chat representation is `role: content`, not
  automatically the model's chat template. Explicit `--chat-template` or
  `--chat-template-file PATH` requires `--tokenizer`; record the tokenizer/template
  identity from the preparation report. Do not silently change formatting between
  calibration, tuning and final testing.
* Direct `quantize --calib` and `eval --text` JSONL input expects a `text` field;
  it is not the same chat-record parser. Preprocess chat before these commands.
  Plain-text output from `calib` is the simplest common input.
* `--tokenizer MODEL` gives real token counts; without it `calib` estimates one
  token per four characters. Mixture weights are token-share goals under
  whole-document selection, not an assurance of exact proportions in every window.

## Allocation and KLD targets

For tensor `t`, row `r` and candidate `c`, let
`e[t,r,c] = sensitivity[t] * candidate_sse[t,r,c] / tensor_energy[t]`
(with the implementation's small-denominator guard). Define:

```text
E(picks) = sum over tensors and rows of e[t,r,picks[t,r]]
E_best   = sum over tensors and rows of min_c e[t,r,c]
quality constraint: E(picks) <= R * E_best
```

`--max-error-ratio R` searches for a low-bit allocation satisfying this **global**
weighted budget ([allocator](../hi2/allocate.py)). Individual rows can exceed R
times their own minimum while the sum meets the constraint. The ratio is
dimensionless and relative to the allowed candidates; changing the candidates
changes the floor. The tables are an output-error proxy, not a KLD measurement,
a per-row bound, or a guaranteed error bound after GPTQ/QAD.

The sensitivity probe normalizes measured relative KLD effects by their median.
If that median is zero but positive effects exist, it uses the positive-effects
median and keeps the configured floor for zero-signal units. An entirely
zero-signal probe keeps neutral unit weights rather than dividing by zero.
Zero measured sensitivity is not proof of losslessness.

`--target-kld K` measures PTQ candidates on tuning text and fits/interpolates a
ratio. The predicted KLD is not a measured score for the final checkpoint.
An out-of-range target may remain unbracketed after extensions; inspect the
reported tuning status and measured points, not just the selected ratio.
QAD and final logit-KL training can improve one corpus and regress another.
The historical [27B fast-recipe regression](qad.md) remains relevant.
A completed final-KL phase is not a final quality result.

Choose acceptance thresholds before inspecting the final corpus. Then measure
the **published final artifact** on that untouched text with `hi2 quality`.
The tuning prediction and internal training scores cannot replace this gate:

```bash
hi2 quality MODEL OUT --text final-test.txt \
  --max-kld "$MAX_KLD" --max-ppl-ratio "$MAX_PPL_RATIO" \
  --samples 8 --seq-len 2048 --device cpu
```

Set `MAX_KLD` and `MAX_PPL_RATIO` to your predeclared acceptance thresholds;
there is no universal recommendation hidden in this example. The gate reports
`target_met`, `target_not_met` or `not_measured`; a gate miss exits nonzero.
Only a measured `target_met` is acceptance. This is dense numerical evaluation,
not native serving validation. Use `quality` instead of a separate `eval` when
you want both a measured result and an explicit threshold decision.

Before scoring, `quality` verifies every recorded `hi2_run.json` calibration input
against its full SHA-256 identity, then checks exact document overlap with the
final corpus. Recorded entries must contain an absolute path and a valid full
SHA-256 digest. Missing files, changed bytes (even with unchanged size/timestamps),
or malformed identities stop evaluation without publishing a new quality report.
Replacing a calibration file with unrelated text cannot make its original
training documents eligible for final evaluation. Keep the original files
available, including when evaluating a relocated portable checkpoint.

Additional `--exclude-data` inputs remain part of the overlap check. Legacy
artifacts without recorded calibration provenance can only check explicitly
supplied exclusions; an empty checked-input list is not evidence that training
or tuning data were disjoint. `semantic_deduplication` remains `not_measured`.
If the original source fingerprint is absent, the report's
`reference.matches_quantization_source` is `null`, not a claim of a match.

The reference identity also includes `source.auxiliary_inputs`, notably the
external table selected by `HI2_NGRAM_MMAP`. `quality` compares the current
canonical path and full SHA-256 against the recorded list before evaluation.
Missing, changed, differently located, or unrecorded external inputs are rejected;
a symlink to the same canonical file is allowed. An absent list means no recorded
auxiliary inputs, so enabling a table for such an artifact is not silently accepted.
An exact match uses the existing dense evaluator's table attachment, and the
report records these identities in `reference.auxiliary_inputs`. Matching only
the model directory fingerprint is insufficient to establish the reference.

Missing, invalid, or nonfinite required measurements produce `not_measured`.
Nonfinite returned metric values are stored as JSON `null`, never replaced with
invented finite scores, and finite measurements are retained. A failed
measurement is published to `hi2_quality.json` before the strict gate raises
`QualityRejected`; the Python API's `strict=False` returns the same rejection report.
For portable outputs, publication also updates the portable manifest so the
rejected result remains structurally verifiable. This is a quality rejection,
not structural acceptance of its numerical result.

Thresholds are corpus/model-specific. `lossless: true` in `eval` means only
`kld_nats_per_token < 0.01` on the measured positions. It does not certify identical
weights, generations, tool choices, task accuracy, or native runtime behavior.

## Read the results

### Verification coverage

`hi2 verify MODEL OUT` reloads stored tensors and compares reconstructed weights
with the matching source. The default `--max-tensors 8` is a **sampled numerical**
check, not whole-model structural validation. The report records checked versus
expected tensor coverage and labels native execution as not run; zero checks or
an incomplete tensor-report mapping cannot establish success.

Complete structural validation is a separate operation implemented by
`hi2.checkpoint.validate_checkpoint`: it scans the shard/index mapping, dense
finite values, HI2 dtypes/shapes/offset bounds/widths/flags and finite decoded
ladders, for either MoE layout, with complete report coverage. The quantization
pipeline invokes it before publishing success. For an independent check:

```bash
hi2 validate OUT
```

Use this all-artifact structural check, not a larger numerical sample count as
a substitute. Structural validity still is not model quality or native loader
validation. A successful command is evidence only when it reports nonempty
expected coverage; retain the report.

Unweighted energy loss is `sum((decoded - source)^2) / sum(source^2)` for a
weight tensor. It is not activation-weighted allocation error, end-to-end KLD,
a task score or a native inference test. Reloaded bpw consistency likewise is
an accounting check, not a quality threshold. Source:
[`checkpoint.py`](../hi2/checkpoint.py), [`stack.py`](../hi2/stack.py).

### Size and bpw

```bash
hi2 size OUT --src MODEL --by kind --top 10
```

* **Allocation bpw** is the allocator's budget/accounting model over eligible
  weights; its compact metadata model is not every byte in the serialized file.
* **Stored linear bpw** is `8 * linears.bytes / linears.weights`, including codes,
  block parameters, row constants, smoothing and row/layout index arrays.
  Precision shares here count **weights**, not the report's fraction of rows.
* **Tensor bytes** include the quantized body and remaining dense tensors.
  Unmodified dense tensors are copied in source dtype; trained overrides may
  replace some norms. They are not universally fp16.
* **File bytes** include the safetensors files and their headers, not necessarily
  every tokenizer/auxiliary asset, cache or workdir. Whole-directory deployment
  footprint additionally includes those assets and any materialized links.
* **Dense export** explicitly writes fp16 dense weights and forfeits compressed
  runtime memory savings. This is distinct from copying source tensors into HI2.

Compare the same parameter set and units across formats. State whether an
embedding, head, vision tower or MTP module is quantized, dense, or excluded.
MiB = 2^20 bytes and GiB = 2^30 bytes: 16 GiB is 16,384 MiB, not 16,000 MiB.
Source: [`size.py`](../hi2/size.py) and [the corrected 27B comparison](results.md#the-quality-side-kld-against-bf16-on-the-same-windows).

### Numerical and task metrics

**PPL** is perplexity; the PPL ratio compares the quantized model with its
reference. **EAR** is the expected acceptance rate computed from distribution
overlap, not a measured serving acceptance rate. **nMSE** is normalized
mean-squared error.

| Metric/check | What it measures | What it does not establish |
| --- | --- | --- |
| PPL and PPL ratio | Next-token likelihood of the chosen text; ratio to the matching reference | Equal PPL does not imply equal distributions or decisions. A ratio below 1 is possible after quantization. |
| KLD | Mean `KL(reference || quantized)` in nats per scored token on that corpus | Not a universal task-accuracy threshold or generation-equivalence certificate. |
| Top-1 agreement | Fraction of evaluated contexts with the same highest-probability token | Not the fraction of full generated answers that will match. |
| EAR | Mean `sum_v min(p_v, q_v)` distribution overlap at evaluated contexts | Not measured speculative-serving acceptance or throughput; scheduling, draft cost and native kernels are absent. |
| Internal QAD nMSE/KL | Training/selection objective on internal validation windows | Not an independent final-corpus score. |
| Task accuracy and decision fidelity | Behavior on the named tasks/prompts and scoring rules | Not universal utility or proof that two sampled runs establish determinism. |
| Native loader plus inference request | The selected runtime actually loads and executes the low-bit checkpoint | Not established by a dense export or `hi2 eval`. Requires its own versioned evidence. |

A **signed log-probability gap** compares reference and candidate log-probabilities
for selected tokens. It can be negative and is not true KLD over the full token
distribution. Do not report such a gap as KLD or apply `--max-kld` thresholds
to it. Use the benchmark harness's metric definitions when comparing task reports
with dense `eval` results.

`eval` exports/dequantizes the checkpoint to dense fp16 HF weights and loads the
original architecture; `--dtype` controls evaluation math, not a native low-bit
kernel. Record model and checkpoint revisions, corpus/split and hash, tokenizer,
offset, scored positions, dtype/devices and evaluator revision. Compare repeated
runs with paired item-level intervals, noting that they do not model every source
of runtime variation. See [`evalq.py`](../hi2/evalq.py).

For task metrics use [bench/README.md](../bench/README.md): the checkout tooling
has task-specific endpoint requirements, optional datasets, incomplete-run
reporting and generated-code execution risks. A generic chat endpoint may not
provide `/tokenize` or prompt log-probabilities. Code-task execution is not a
security sandbox; read the safety guidance before enabling it.


<!-- docs/format-spec.md -->
# HadamardINT2-FP16 v3.2

Status: **published serialization reference for the implemented v3.2 format**.
Section 2 defines the normative tensor representation and decode semantics;
the [stacked MoE names, arrays and invariants](moe.md#stacked-expert-layout---moe-layout-stacked)
extend its container. [`hi2/format.py`](../hi2/format.py),
[`hi2/checkpoint.py`](../hi2/checkpoint.py) and [`hi2/stack.py`](../hi2/stack.py)
are the corresponding implementation. Section 10 records changes from v3.1.

The remaining sections record encoder policy, dated experiments, hardware
observations and explicitly identified proposals; they are not additional
serialization requirements or a guarantee that a particular runtime supports
the format. Section 12 is the evaluation protocol for this document's research
claims, not a loader requirement. The design study in section 11 is non-normative.
The original measurements in sections 3, 4 and 6 came from `experiments/` scripts
or gfx1030 V620 probes; no new GPU validation is implied by this documentation.

| Property | Scope |
| --- | --- |
| Scope | Weight matrices. Activation handling is defined only for the dot-product input. KV cache is out of scope. |
| Hardware evidence | RDNA2 `gfx1030`, ROCm 7.14 toolchain; see each measurement's conditions. |
| Runtime boundary | Native loader/kernels live in an external vLLM fork; calibration lives in `hi2`. Historical serving results do not identify a newly qualified runtime revision. |
| Format name | `rdna_hint_flex` (`quant_method`; "HI2" in this document), tensor prefix `hi2_`, mode `flex`. Historical runtime names such as `hadamard_int2` are not an alternative emitted schema or a promise of alias support in other loaders. |

## 1. What changed from v3.1, in one table

| Topic | v3.1 | v3.2 | Evidence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Block parameters | two fp16 per block (`lo`, `step`) | two **8-bit** per block, relative to per-row fp16 constants | no measurable quality change, saves 0.25-1.0 bpw of block parameters (0.5 at B=32) before the 48/K row constants (4.1) |
| Block sizes | 32, 64 (16 dropped: 2 bpw of ladder) | **16, 32, 64** (B=16 costs 1.0 bpw with 8-bit params) | B=16 beats mixing INT2/INT4 rows at ~3.0 bpw in layer-output error (4.3); no end-to-end gain shown yet (4.5) |
| Compute | one path, `v_dot2_f32_f16` | **two modes** on identical stored weights: F16 (`v_dot2`) and I8 (`v_dot4_i32_i8`) | 6 |
| Activation | fp16 | I8 mode: int8 with an fp16 scale per **16** columns | adds ~0.6% output error vs 19-26% from INT2 weights (5) |
| Hadamard | mandatory | **rotation plus smoothing** (default on); rotation alone is not enough | smoothing then rotation helps at every bit width (-30% perplexity at 2.5 bpw); rotation alone hurts a calibrated encoder but helps a data-free one by 31% (4.2, 4.5, 4.6) |
| Encoder | min-max | calibrator-side: least-squares `lo/step` fit, activation-weighted | -39% output error vs min-max at INT2/B32 (4.2) |
| Rounding | nearest | **Hessian-aware error-compensated rounding (GPTQ), default when calibrated** (8.3); offline only, no format or kernel change | removes 66% of the perplexity excess at 3.0 bpw on Qwen3.5-0.8B (1.92 to 1.32) and 57% on Qwen2.5-0.5B (4.8) |
| ISA table | partly wrong | verified by assembler and hardware run | 7 |

## 2. Format

### 2.1 Tensor and blocks

`W[R, K]`. Blocks run along K, size `B` in {16, 32, 64}, chosen per row.
The serialized row width is **INT2, INT4 or INT8** (`nbits` in {2, 4, 8};
4, 16 or 256 code levels respectively). `K` must be a multiple of 64.

Serialized support is distinct from encoder search policy. The current encoder
defaults to INT2/INT4 with B=16/32/64; `--bits 2,4,8` additionally permits
INT8/B32 and INT8/B64 candidates ([`calibrate.SELECTABLE`](../hi2/calibrate.py)).
INT8 is not enabled by default and does not require a different container.

### 2.2 Affine ladder with hierarchical parameters

Per block the reconstruction is `w = lo + code * step`, `code` in `0..levels-1`.
Rather than storing `lo` and `step` as fp16 per block, each row stores three
fp16 constants and each block stores two bytes:

```text
row:    lo_min, lo_scale, step_scale        (fp16)
block:  lo_u8, step_u8                      (uint8)

lo   = fp16( fma_f32(lo_u8,  lo_scale,   lo_min) )
step = fp16( step_u8 * step_scale )          (fp32 multiply, one rounding to fp16)
w    = fp16( lo + code * step )              (exact in fp64, one rounding; v_fma_f16)
```

`step_u8 = 0` is the degenerate block (constant `lo`). The quantizer **must
encode codes from the decoded fp16 `lo` and `step`**, so offline and device
agree bit for bit. Per-row constants cost `48 / K` bits per weight (0.012 bpw at
K=4096).

Why not a symmetric ladder (`step` only, levels centred): measured worse at equal
bpw (4.1). Why not nvfp4-style FP8 scales: a linear 8-bit step relative to the
row maximum already costs nothing measurable, so there is nothing to recover.

### 2.3 Smoothing and rotation

Two optional transforms act on the input dimension, in this order, on the weights
offline and on the activation at runtime.

**Smoothing** (needs calibration statistics): a per-column fp16 vector `s` with
`s_j = (d_j / geomean(d)) ** (alpha / 2)`, `d_j = E[x_j^2]` and `alpha = 0.5`
(range 0.05 to 20). The weights become `W * s` and the activation `x / s`, so the
product is unchanged. This equalises the channel scales the encoder would otherwise
have to prioritise.

**Rotation**: orthonormal Walsh-Hadamard of order 16 on groups of 16 consecutive
columns, same flags for every row (`K/16` bits per tensor, or 1 bit if uniform).
`H H = I`, so the dot product is preserved exactly; only where quantization error
lands changes.

The stored basis is therefore `H (W * s)` and the runtime activation is `H (x / s)`.
With no smoothing vector `s = 1`. Rotation alone, with an encoder that weights
columns by activation statistics, makes the model worse (it mixes the channel
scales the encoder exploits); rotation after smoothing, or rotation with an
encoder that has no activation statistics, helps (4.5, 4.6). The flags can be all
zero or mixed; the default is all ones.

### 2.4 Layout

Flat per-row buffers as in v3.1 section 6.1 (`codes`, `row_code_off`, `row_nbits`,
`row_block`, `had`), with these replacing `lo` / `step` / `row_scale_off`:

| Buffer | Type | Length |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `blk_params` | `uint8` pairs `(lo_u8, step_u8)` | `2 * sum_r K/B_r` |
| `row_consts` | `fp16` triple | `3 * R` |
| `row_code_off` | `int32`, in 32-bit words | `R+1` |
| `row_param_off` | `int32`, in blocks (each block is one `(lo_u8, step_u8)` pair) | `R+1` |
| `col_scale` (optional) | `fp16` | `K` |

Codes are packed LSB-first in 32-bit words as before (16 INT2, 8 INT4 or 4 INT8 per word). The checkpoint tensors are `hi2_code_off` and `hi2_param_off` (the offsets are `int32`, as written by the tool and read by the fork's kernels).

### 2.5 Bit accounting

The allocation model is `nbits + 16/B + 48/K`, plus modeled metadata
(Hadamard bits, row-width and block-size flags) and `16/R` for a smoothing
vector. At R=4096 smoothing costs 0.004 bpw, but at R=128 it costs 0.125 bpw.
This is **not the actual serialized size**: stored offsets and per-row arrays
have the dtypes in the container, rather than bit-packed theoretical flags.
Use [`hi2 size`](evaluation.md#size-and-bpw) for actual tensor bytes, body bpw
and whole-checkpoint comparisons. Shared-input transforms can have the same
values without being stored only once; folding them into a norm is not the
current serialized representation.

| nbits | B=16 | B=32 | B=64 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2 | 3.012 | 2.512 | 2.262 |
| 4 | 5.012 | 4.512 | 4.262 |
| 8 | 9.012 | 8.512 | 8.262 |

(K=4096, metadata excluded. The saving over v3.1's fp16 pair is 0.98 bpw at B=16,
0.48 at B=32 and 0.23 at B=64.)

## 3. How these numbers were measured

Model Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct (bf16 weights, fp32 math), 5 layers (0, 6, 12, 18,
23) x {q, o, up, down} = 20 matrices, 256 sampled rows each. Activations are
captured by hooking the real forward pass on 4096 tokens of repo documentation.
**The encoder is calibrated on the first 2048 tokens and every error below is
measured on the other 2048** (held out). Metric: relative L2 error of the layer
output `X W_hat^T` against `X W^T`, averaged over matrices. This experiment measures
output error, **not KL divergence or perplexity**; later model-level results below
and in [results.md](results.md) use those other metrics.
One small model: treat the *ordering* of options as the finding, not the
absolute values. Script `experiments/scale_study.py`; raw results
`experiments/results/qwen2.5-0.5b-scale-study.json`.

## 4. Scale system, encoder, rotation

### 4.1 Scale system (activation-weighted encoder, no rotation, INT2)

| System | B=16 | B=32 | B=64 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| affine, fp16 `lo`+`step` (v3.1) | 4.000 bpw / 0.1905 | 3.000 / 0.2297 | 2.500 / 0.2606 |
| **affine, 8-bit params (v3.2)** | **3.000 / 0.1907** | **2.500 / 0.2296** | **2.250 / 0.2604** |
| symmetric, fp16 `step` | 3.000 / 0.2592 | 2.500 / 0.2844 | 2.250 / 0.3051 |
